MCGDM approach based on (p, q, r)-spherical fuzzy Frank aggregation operators: applications in the categorization of renewable energy sources
Authors: Tmader Alballa, Muhammad Rahim, Alhanouf Alburaikan, A. Almutairi, Hamiden Abd El-Wahed Khalifa
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
The growing demand for energy, driven by population growth and technological advancements, has made ensuring a sufficient and sustainable energy supply a critical challenge for humanity. Renewable energy sources, such as biomass, solar, wind, and hydro, are inexhaustible and environmentally friendly, offering a viable solution to both the energy crisis and the fight against global warming. However, selecting the optimal renewable energy source remains a complex decision-making problem due to the varying characteristics and impacts of these sources. Motivated by the need for more accurate and nuanced decision-making tools in this domain, this paper introduces a novel multicriteria group decision-making (MCGDM) approach based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy Frank aggregation operators. By integrating Frank t-norm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy sets, we develop aggregation operators (AOs) that effectively manage membership, neutral, and non-membership degrees through parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document}. These AOs provide a more refined framework for decision-making, leading to improved outcomes. We apply this approach to evaluate and identify the superior and optimal renewable energy source using artificial data, demonstrating the advantages of the proposed operators compared to existing methods. This work contributes to the field by offering a robust tool for addressing the energy crisis and advancing sustainable energy solutions.

Introduction

Energy production from abundant natural resources is achievable through sustainable energy technologies. These resources are theoretically endless or replenish more quickly than they are consumed. Sustainable energy sources are often compared to those that rely on fossil fuels. Fossil fuels such as coal, oil, and natural gas are limited and unsustainable over the long term. In contrast, sustainable energies originate from natural resources such as solar radiation, wind, and water cycles, which are deemed inexhaustible within a human lifetime. These energy sources are frequently referred to as “green” or “clean” energy due to their minimal environmental impact. Compared to fossil fuels, sustainable energy sources are gaining significance in corporate social responsibility (CSR) strategies that promote sustainable development. Various sustainable energy sources, including solar, wind, and hydro, produce different amounts of renewable energy. Recently, the adoption of sustainable energy has significantly increased. In 2017, these resources contributed to 8% of global electricity consumption and now constitute one-third of Europe’s power sector. The transition to sustainable energy is crucial for reducing dependence on finite fossil fuels and minimizing environmental impacts. Incorporating these renewable energy sources into the energy mix ensures a more sustainable and resilient energy future. Simultaneously, one-six of the total energy grid intake occurs in the United State of America, India, and Japan, while China alone receives a quarter of it. As the world’s population continues to increase, the need for energy to support homes, businesses, and communities grows. Ensuring a sustainable energy equilibrium and mitigating climate changes necessitates the advancement and expansion of renewable energy sources. Wang et al.1 conducted a study on the impact of renewable energy, while Ang et al.2 explored renewable energy sources. Abban et al.3 discussed the economic ramifications of renewable energy development in African oil-producing nations. Additionally, Wang et al.4 delved into various strategies for advancing renewable energy initiatives.

Fuzzy sets (FS)5 are mathematical tool for handling uncertainty by allowing elements to belong to a set to varying degrees, rather than strictly yes or no. Over time scholars have leveraged FS to examine renewable energy systems from diverse perspectives. Suganthi et al.6 explored renewable energy systems through the lens of fuzzy logic, Shahnazi and Alimohammadlou7 explored risk factors associated with renewable energy in oil-producing using decision-making methods within fuzzy sets. Heo et al.8 evaluated renewable energy attributes within an FS framework. Colak and Kaya9 scrutinized alternative selection strategies for renewable energy based on FS, and Solangi et al.10 identified obstacles utilizing fuzzy methodologies. Sengul et al.11 delved into energy supply systems and their hierarchical evaluation using fuzzy decision-making techniques, whereas Solangi et al.12 investigated renewable energy sources in developing countries employing fuzzy decision methodologies.

On limitation of FSs is their exclusive focus on membership degrees (MDs), neglecting non-membership degrees (NMDs). This oversight can lead to an incomplete representation of uncertainty, as FSs fail to account for degrees of NMDs or exclusion. Consequently, situations where an element does not belong to a set are not adequately addressed, potentially limiting the accuracy and comprehensiveness of fuzzy set-based models and decision-making processes. To address these limitations, Atanassov13 introduced intuitionistic fuzzy sets (IFSs), which consider both MDs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{U}$$\end{document}) and NMDs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{V}$$\end{document}) simultaneously, under the condition that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{V}\le\:1$$\end{document}. This extension allows for more comprehensive depiction of hesitation, accommodating situations where elements may partially belong to a set while also partially not belonging. By incorporating both MDs and MNDs, IFSs provide a more nuanced and accurate framework for modeling uncertain data. Over the years, a multitude of scholars have investigated renewable energy perspectives of IFSs. For example, Joshi et al.14 introduced a MCGDM method under IFSs for determining the optimal renewable energy source. Majumder et al.15 introduced an extensive method under IFSs for evaluating the viability of solar plant projects. Bilgili et al.16 conducted an analysis on the most suitable renewable energy options for Turkey’s sustainable development using the intuitionistic fuzzy TOPSIS method. Davoudabadi et al.17 introduced a decision model utilizing Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA) and simulation techniques to assess renewable energy projects within the framework of interval valued IFSs. Nithyanandham et al.18 developed an energy-based MCDM technique using bipolar intuitionistic fuzzy digraphs, applying it to select the best COVID-19 vaccine, and validated the method through comparative and sensitivity analyses. Jana et al.19 introduced a linguistic interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy QUALIFLEX method with a likelihood-based comparison approach. Palanikumar et al.20 proposed the concept of a logarithmic square root neutrosophic normal interval-valued set for addressing MCDM problems. Riaz et al.21 introduced the concept of linear Diophantine fuzzy sets (LDFSs) to relax the conditions imposed on IFSs Pythagorean fuzzy sets22 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q-$$\end{document}rung orthopair fuzzy sets23. To ease strict constraints of existing fuzzy extensions, Riaz et al.24 introduced spherical LDFSs with reference parameters, which are useful for modeling uncertainties in MCDM. Recognizing the flexibility of linear Diophantine fuzzy sets, scholars have conducted various studies, as noted in references25–27.

A limitation IFS is that they account for MD and NMD but overlook the neutral membership (NEMD) of an object during evaluation. Consider a restaurant menu where dishes are categorized based on their spiciness level. A dish may be categorized as “mild,” “medium,” or “hot.” However, some dishes may have a moderate spiciness level that does not fit precisely into any of these categories. This intermediate spiciness level represents a NEMD, but IFS may overlook this nuanced classification. To address such type of scenarios, Cuong and Krepinevich28 introduced picture fuzzy sets (PFSs), which address the limitation of IFSs by simultaneously considering MD, NMD and NEMD (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{E}$$\end{document}), under the condition that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{V}\le\:1$$\end{document}. Various extensions of PFSs have been proposed by different researchers. For instance, Gundogdu and Kahraman29 introduced spherical fuzzy sets (SFSs) as an alternative to address the limitations of PFSs, offering a relaxation of the conditions imposed by PFSs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{V}\le\:1$$\end{document}). Mahmood et al.30 proposed t-spherical fuzzy sets (t-SFSs) under the condition that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathcal{U}}^{t}+{\mathcal{E}}^{t}+{\mathcal{V}}^{t}\le\:1$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:t\ge\:1$$\end{document}. These extensions, such as picture fuzzy sets and spherical fuzzy sets, find applications in various fields of renewable energy. For example, in31, optimal site selection for renewable energy projects32, modeling uncertainty in renewable energy production forecasts33, and decision-making processes related to investment in renewable energy technologies34–37.

The evaluation of alternatives is a crucial stage, where decision-makers may employ various term levels to handle the impact of MD, NMD, and NEMD. However, PFS, SFS, and t-SFS do not permit decision-makers to utilize different term levels for membership degrees. For instance, in PFSs, decision-makers are restricted to term level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:1$$\end{document} for all membership degrees, while SFSs allow term level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:2$$\end{document} for all membership degrees, and in t-SFS, decision-makers can utilize term level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:t$$\end{document} (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:t$$\end{document} is any positive integer) for all membership degrees during alternative assessment. To address the limitations imposed on these sets, Rahim et al.38 introduced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy sets (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs), enabling decision-makers to employ various term levels for membership degrees in alternative assessment. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs offer distinct advantages in decision-making processes by allowing decision-makers to utilize different term levels for membership degrees, thus enhancing the representation of uncertainty. Parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document} controls the MDs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document} manages neutral NEMDs, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} governs NMDs such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathcal{U}}^{p}+{\mathcal{E}}^{r}+{\mathcal{V}}^{q}\le\:1$$\end{document}, here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} are positive integers and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r=\text{max}(p,q)$$\end{document}. This flexibility enables more comprehensive assessments of alternatives, accommodating various degrees of ambiguity and vagueness, and facilitating more informed and precise evaluations. To grasp the distinctions among PFSs, SFSs, t-SFS, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFS, refer to Fig. 1.

Fig. 1Spaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy set for different arrangements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document}

Spaces of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy set for different arrangements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document}

AOs offer significant advantages in data fusion and decision-making processes. AOs have emerged as a prominent area of research, yielding a wide array of investigation outcomes that continue to garner significant interest. Shahab et al.39 proposed spherical fuzzy AOs to improve decision-making in sustainable energy planning. Riaz et al.40 introduced Einstein weighted averaging operators for optimal selection of renewable energy resources. Farid et al.41 introduced t-spherical fuzzy AOs and explored their practical implementation in DM scenarios in real-world contexts. Garg et al.42 proposed sine trigonometric AOs in t-spherical fuzzy environment.

The advantages of Frank t-norm and t-conorm43 in aggregation include their flexibility, parameter adjustability, and compatibility with fuzzy logic operations, ensuring precise control over the aggregation process and enhancing system functionality. Various AOs have been introduced in the literature, utilizing the Frank t-norm and t-conorm. As an illustration, Naeem et al.44 introduced a series of Frank AOs within the framework of bipolar complex fuzzy sets for the classification of renewable energy sources. Seikh and Mandal45 introduced q-rung orthopair fuzzy Frank AOs and examined their utility in decision-making scenarios. Readers can refer to references46–50 for additional Frank AOs applied in various contexts to address real-world decision-making challenges. However, there is no research on the aggregation operator using t-norm and t-conorm using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left(p,q,r\right)-$$\end{document}SFSs.

Despite the extensive research on aggregation operators within various fuzzy set frameworks, there is a notable lack of studies focusing on the development and application of aggregation operators based on t-norm and t-conorm functions within the context of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy sets (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs). This gap is particularly significant given the potential of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs to provide more flexible and accurate decision-making tools by accommodating multiple degrees of membership, non-membership, and indeterminacy. The absence of such research highlights an opportunity to explore how t-norm and t-conorm-based aggregation operators can enhance the effectiveness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs in complex decision-making scenarios, particularly in fields like renewable energy, where nuanced and reliable assessments are crucial.

Renewable energy sources, such as solar and wind power, are naturally variable and subject to fluctuations caused by weather conditions and seasonal changes. This variability means that their availability and performance can fluctuate over time. Additionally, the criteria used to assess and classify renewable energy sources may also change based on evolving technological advancements, regulatory policies, and environmental considerations. As a result, static assessment methods or AOs may not fully capture the dynamic nature of renewable energy sources, or the evolving criteria used for evaluation. Therefore, it is essential to develop adaptable and robust assessment frameworks that can accommodate these changes and provide accurate evaluations of renewable energy sources over time. The paper describes the development of AOs based on the Frank t-norm and t-conorm, considering the structure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs. These operators are designed to aggregate information represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs, which are fuzzy sets characterized by their MDs, NDMs, and NEMDs. This approach allows for more accurate and comprehensive aggregation of fuzzy information, enhancing decision-making processes or analysis tasks that involve uncertain or imprecise data.

The contribution of the presented work as follows:

The deduction of two AOs within the framework of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF) information, namely the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF Frank weighted averaging (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWA) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF Frank weighted geometric (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWG) operators. These operators serve as essential tools for amalgamating information represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs, thereby enhancing the efficacy of analysis and decision-making processes in domains characterized by the prevalence of such fuzzy information.Utilizing the newly devised operators, we formulate a decision-making approach tailored for handling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF information in practical decision-making scenarios.Given the significance of renewable energy, our study delves into its exploration, aiming to identify the optimal source of renewable energy. The deduction of two AOs within the framework of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}spherical fuzzy (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF) information, namely the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF Frank weighted averaging (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWA) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF Frank weighted geometric (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWG) operators. These operators serve as essential tools for amalgamating information represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs, thereby enhancing the efficacy of analysis and decision-making processes in domains characterized by the prevalence of such fuzzy information.

Utilizing the newly devised operators, we formulate a decision-making approach tailored for handling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF information in practical decision-making scenarios.

Utilizing the newly devised operators, we formulate a decision-making approach tailored for handling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF information in practical decision-making scenarios.

Given the significance of renewable energy, our study delves into its exploration, aiming to identify the optimal source of renewable energy. We accomplish this by analyzing artificial data structured under the framework of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFS and employing our developed decision-making approach.

Given the significance of renewable energy, our study delves into its exploration, aiming to identify the optimal source of renewable energy. We accomplish this by analyzing artificial data structured under the framework of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFS and employing our developed decision-making approach.

The layout of the paper is shown in Fig. 2.

The flowchart depicting the established work.

Preliminaries

In this section, we present fundamental definitions, concepts, and operational laws essential for the research conducted herein. These foundational elements serve as the groundwork upon which our study is built, providing the necessary framework for subsequent analysis and investigation.

Score and accuracy functions play vital roles in evaluating fuzzy-based information. Score functions assign numerical scores to fuzzy-based predictions or classifications, reflecting the confidence level associated with each prediction. These scores facilitate comparisons and help assess the reliability of fuzzy-based outputs. Accuracy functions, on the other hand, provide a quantitative measure of the overall correctness of fuzzy-based classifications by comparing them to known or ground truth values. By utilizing both functions, practitioners can effectively gauge the performance and reliability of fuzzy-based models and decision-making systems, enabling informed decision-making and optimization efforts. The mathematical formulation for the score and accuracy functions is provided in Eqs. (2) and (3).

38 Let be a SFN. The score value of can be calculate as:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:SC\left(\vartheta\:\right)={\mathcal{U}}^{p}-{\mathcal{E}}^{r}-{\mathcal{V}}^{q}$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document} are positive integers such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:0\le\:SC\left(\vartheta\:\right)\le\:1$$\end{document}.

Definition 6

43 Frank t-norm and Frank t-conorm can be expressed as follows:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)={\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{{\Omega\:}}_{1}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{{\Omega\:}}_{2}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)\:\text{f}\text{o}\text{r}\:\text{a}\text{l}\text{l}\:\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)\in\:{\left[\text{0,1}\right]}^{2}$$\end{document}5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)=1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{{\Omega\:}}_{1}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{1-{\Omega\:}}_{2}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)\:\text{f}\text{o}\text{r}\:\text{a}\text{l}\text{l}\:\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)\in\:{\left[\text{0,1}\right]}^{2}$$\end{document}

It is noted that the Eqs. (4) and (5) exhibit the following properties51:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)+{S}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)={{\Omega\:}}_{1}+{{\Omega\:}}_{2}$$\end{document}7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\frac{\partial\:{T}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)}{\partial\:{{\Omega\:}}_{1}}+\frac{\partial\:{S}_{F}\left({{\Omega\:}}_{1},{{\Omega\:}}_{2}\right)}{\partial\:{{\Omega\:}}_{1}}$$\end{document}

In Eq. (6), the sum of the t-norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{F}$$\end{document}​ and t-conorm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{F}$$\end{document}​ applied to the fuzzy sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Omega\:}}_{1}$$\end{document}​ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Omega\:}}_{2}$$\end{document}​ is consistent with the direct sum of these sets. The partial derivatives in Eq. (7) highlight how small changes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Omega\:}}_{1}$$\end{document}​ impact the combined outcomes of the t-norm and t-co-norm.

Novel operational law and AOs

In this section, we introduce fundamental operational laws leveraging the Frank t-norm, t-conorm, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFSs. These laws and their associated properties are elaborated upon in “Operational laws”. Building upon these operational laws, we introduce two AOs, namely the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFFWG operators, designed to aggregate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SF information. The properties of these AOs are discussed in detail in “Proposed AOs”.

Operational laws

Theorem 1

Let, , and be any three SFNs, and, and are positive real numbers, then

i. , \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\vartheta\:}_{1}{\oplus\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{2}={\vartheta\:}_{2}{\oplus\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{1}$$\end{document}

ii. ,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\vartheta\:}_{1}{\otimes\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{2}={\vartheta\:}_{2}{\otimes\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{1}$$\end{document}

iii. ,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\eta\:}_{1}{\cdot\:}_{F}\vartheta\:\oplus\:{\eta\:}_{2}{\cdot\:}_{F}\vartheta\:=({\eta\:}_{1}+{\eta\:}_{2})\vartheta\:$$\end{document}

iv. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\vartheta\:}^{\wedge}_{F}{\eta\:}_{1}\otimes\:{\vartheta\:}^{\wedge}_{F}{\eta\:}_{2}={\vartheta\:}^{\wedge}_{F}{\eta\:}_{1}+{\eta\:}_{2}$$\end{document} .

Proof

By Definition (1), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\vartheta\:}_{1}{\oplus\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{p}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{p}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{r}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{r}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\sqrt[q]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{q}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{2}^{q}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{p}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{p}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{r}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{r}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\sqrt[q]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{2}^{q}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{q}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)}\end{array}\right)={\vartheta\:}_{1}{\oplus\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{2}$$\end{document} .

Similarly,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{p}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{p}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{r}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{r}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[q]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{q}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{V}}_{2}^{q}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{p}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{p}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{r}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{r}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[q]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+\frac{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{V}}_{2}^{q}}-1\right)\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{q}}-1\right)}{\rho\:-1}\right)}\end{array}\right)={\vartheta\:}_{1}{\otimes\:}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{2}$$\end{document}.

Parts (iii) and (iv) can be proven in a similar manner.

Remark 1

Some special case.

Proposed AOs

The definitions and operational laws presented in “Operational laws” are used to define the following AOs and their properties.

Definition 8

Let (where ) be a group of SFNs and is the weight vector of these SFNs such that and. Then the SFFWA operator can be defined as follows:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-SFFWA\left({\vartheta\:}_{1},{\vartheta\:}_{2},\dots\:,{\vartheta\:}_{n}\right)={\oplus\:}_{i=1}^{n}{\omega\:}_{i}{.}_{F}{\vartheta\:}_{i}$$\end{document}

In Eq. (8), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}_{i}$$\end{document} represent the weight vector such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}_{i}\in\:\left[\text{0,1}\right]$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sum\:}_{i=1}^{n}{\omega\:}_{i}=1$$\end{document}.

Theorem 2

Assume (where ) be a group of SFNs and is the weight vector of these SFNs such that and. Then the aggregated value obtained by SFFWA operator is also SFNs and can be expressed as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-SFFWA\left({\vartheta\:}_{1},{\vartheta\:}_{2},\dots\:,{\vartheta\:}_{n}\right)={\omega\:}_{1}{\vartheta\:}_{1}{\oplus\:}_{F}{\omega\:}_{2}{\vartheta\:}_{2}{\oplus\:}_{F}\dots\:{\oplus\:}_{F}{\omega\:}_{n}{\vartheta\:}_{n}$$\end{document}9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{i=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{p}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{i}}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{i=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{r}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{i}}\right)},\sqrt[q]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{i=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{i}^{q}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{i}}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document} are positive integers such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r=\text{max}(p,q)$$\end{document}.

Proof

To prove Theorem 2, we utilize mathematical induction.

Step 1. \begin{document}$$\:=\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{i=1}^{2}{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{p}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{i}}\right)}$$\end{document}.

Similarly.

An inherent advantage of the proposed AOs lies in their parametric design, offering flexibility and adaptability in decision-making scenarios. With three parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document} decision-makers can effectively manage membership degrees during the aggregation process, tailoring the operators to specific conditions or requirements. This parametric nature empowers decision-makers to choose suitable values for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document}, allowing for precise adjustment and optimization of the aggregation outcome to best align with the decision context. Based on these operators we constructed the following MCGDM approach.

Proposed MCGDM approach

MCGDM is an essential field in operations research and management science that addresses the complexities of modern decision-making by evaluating multiple, often conflicting criteria. It integrates both quantitative and qualitative factors, structuring decision problems to derive insights that align with decision-makers’ objectives and preferences. A critical aspect of MCDM is the assignment of weights to criteria and alternatives, which reflect their relative importance and influence the robustness of decisions. The suggested MCDM method includes the following steps.

Step 1. Identifications.

Identify the potential alternatives and their associated criteria in a DM problem based on the perspectives of the decision-makers. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\Xi\:}=\left\{{{\Xi\:}}_{2},{{\Xi\:}}_{2},\dots\:,{{\Xi\:}}_{m}\right\}$$\end{document} represent the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m$$\end{document} alternatives, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\Psi\:}=\left\{{{\Psi\:}}_{1},{{\Psi\:}}_{2},\dots\:,{{\Psi\:}}_{n}\right\}$$\end{document} denote the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:n$$\end{document} attributes, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\Theta\:}=\left\{{{\Theta\:}}_{1},{{\Theta\:}}_{2},\dots\:,{{\Theta\:}}_{k}\right\}$$\end{document} indicate the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:k$$\end{document} decision-makers (experts).

Step 2. Data collection.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{M}^{x}={\left({\psi\:}_{ij}^{x}\right)}_{mn}$$\end{document} be the decision matrix provided by the decision maker \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Theta\:}}_{x}$$\end{document}. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\psi\:}_{ij}^{x}=\left({\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{x}\right)$$\end{document} is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFN representing the evaluation value of alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Xi\:}}_{i}$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:i=\text{1,2},\dots\:,m$$\end{document}) concerning criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Psi\:}}_{j}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(j=\text{1,2},\dots\:,n)$$\end{document}, as specified by decision maker \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Theta\:}}_{x}$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:x=\text{1,2},\dots\:,k$$\end{document}). The evaluating values of alternatives, represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\psi\:}_{ij}^{x}=\left({\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{x}\right)$$\end{document}, are organized in the decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{M}^{x}$$\end{document} as follows:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{M}^{x}=\left(\begin{array}{ccc}\begin{array}{c}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{11}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{11}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{11}^{x}\right)\\\:\left({\mathcal{U}}_{21}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{21}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{21}^{x}\right)\end{array}&\:\begin{array}{cc}\begin{array}{c}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{12}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{12}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{12}^{x}\right)\\\:\left({\mathcal{U}}_{22}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{22}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{22}^{x}\right)\end{array}&\:\begin{array}{c}\cdots\:\\\:\cdots\:\end{array}\end{array}&\:\begin{array}{c}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{1n}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{1n}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{1n}^{x}\right)\\\:\left({\mathcal{U}}_{2n}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{2n}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{2n}^{x}\right)\end{array}\\\: \vdots&\:\begin{array}{cc}\vdots&\:\ddots\:\end{array}&\:\vdots\\\:\left({\mathcal{U}}_{m1}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{m1}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{m1}^{x}\right)&\:\begin{array}{cc}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{m2}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{m2}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{m2}^{x}\right)&\:\cdots\:\end{array}&\:\left({\mathcal{U}}_{mn}^{x},{\mathcal{E}}_{mn}^{x},{\mathcal{V}}_{mn}^{x}\right)\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

The rows of the decision matrix presented in Eq. (13), represent the various alternatives, while the columns correspond to the criteria considered by the experts involved in the DM process. Each cell in the matrix contains the rating value for a particular alternative under a specific criterion, reflecting the comprehensive evaluation conducted by the experts.

Step 3. Normalization.

There are two types of criteria: cost criteria (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:C$$\end{document}) and benefit criteria (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:B$$\end{document}). For a well-informed decision, it is essential to convert the cost criteria into benefit criteria. This conversion is achieved using the formula provided in Eq. (14). This step ensures that all criteria are uniformly evaluated, facilitating a more accurate and comprehensive comparison of alternatives.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{r}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}{c}\left({\mathcal{U}}_{ij},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij},{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}\right)\:\:\:\:\:if\:j\in\:B\\\:\left({\mathcal{V}}_{ij},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij},{\mathcal{U}}_{ij}\right)\:\:\:\:\:if\:j\in\:C\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

If all criteria are of the benefit type, normalization is not necessary, and Step 3 of the DM process can be omitted. This simplification streamlines the evaluation process, allowing for a direct comparison of alternatives based on their benefit criteria without additional transformations.

Step 4. Selection of parameters\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varvec{p}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varvec{q}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varvec{r}$$\end{document}

The selection of parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r$$\end{document} is a crucial step in the proposed decision-making process. At this stage, we recommend choosing the minimum values for these parameters such that the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\mathcal{U}}_{ij}}^{p}+{{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}}^{r}+{{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}}^{q}\le\:1$$\end{document} is satisfied. This careful selection ensures the feasibility and accuracy of the DM model. Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:p$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q$$\end{document} are positive integers and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:r=\text{max}(p,q)$$\end{document}.

It is important to explain that these parameters can be chosen based on the specific requirements of the DM process. Additionally, the decision maker has the flexibility to define new rules for selecting these parameters, tailoring the process to better suit their unique needs and objectives.

Step 5. Determination of criteria weights.

For calculating the weights of criteria represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFNs, based on an entropy method which can handle the inherent uncertainty and indeterminacy in the data. Given the criteria \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{r}_{ij}=\left({\mathcal{U}}_{ij},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij},{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}\right)$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:i=\text{1,2},\dots\:,m$$\end{document}; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:j=\text{1,2},\dots\:,n$$\end{document}):

Phase 1. Calculate the entropy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathbb{E}}_{\varvec{j}}$$\end{document}for each criterion:15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathbb{E}}_{j}=\frac{1}{\text{l}\text{n}\left(n\right)}{\sum\:}_{j=1}^{n}\left(\begin{array}{c}{\mathcal{U}}_{j}ln\left({\mathcal{U}}_{j}\right)+\left(1-{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\right)ln\left(1-{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\right)\\\:+{\mathcal{E}}_{j}ln\left({\mathcal{E}}_{j}\right)+\left(1-{\mathcal{E}}_{j}\right)ln\left(1-{\mathcal{E}}_{j}\right)\\\:+{\mathcal{V}}_{j}ln\left({\mathcal{V}}_{j}\right)+(1-{\mathcal{V}}_{j})ln\left(1-{\mathcal{V}}_{j}\right)\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

Phase 2. Calculate the degree of divergence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathcal{D}}_{\varvec{j}}$$\end{document}of each criterion:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\mathcal{D}}_{j}=1-{\mathbb{E}}_{j}$$\end{document}

Phase 3. Calculate the weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\varvec{\omega\:}}_{\varvec{j}}$$\end{document}:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}_{j}=\frac{{\mathcal{D}}_{j}}{{\sum\:}_{j=1}^{n}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{W}\text{h}\text{e}\text{r}\text{e}\:{\omega\:}_{j}\in\:\left[\text{0,1}\right]\:\text{a}\text{n}\text{d}\:\sum\:_{j=1}^{n}{\omega\:}_{j}=1.$$\end{document}

Step 6. Aggregation process.

If two or more decision makers participate in the DM process, their individual decision matrices can be combined using the proposed AOs to form a group decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\stackrel{\sim}{M}={\left({\dot{\psi\:}}_{ij}\right)}_{mn}$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\zeta\:=\left({\zeta\:}_{1},{\zeta\:}_{2},\dots\:,{\zeta\:}_{k}\right)$$\end{document} be the weight vector of decision makers, then18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\dot{\psi\:}}_{ij}=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{x=1}^{k}{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{p}}-1\right)}^{{\zeta\:}_{x}}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{x=1}^{k}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{r}}-1\right)}^{{\zeta\:}_{x}}\right)},\sqrt[q]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{x=1}^{k}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{q}}-1\right)}^{{\zeta\:}_{x}}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

In Eq. (18) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\zeta\:}_{x}$$\end{document} represent the weight vector of criteria.

Step 7. Weighted decision matrix.

Utilize the criteria weights calculated in Step 5 and the group decision matrix to determine the weighted decision matrix using Eq. (19).19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\stackrel{\sim}{WM}={\left({{\omega\:}_{j}\dot{\psi\:}}_{ij}\right)}_{mn}$$\end{document}

Where

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\omega\:}_{j}\dot{\psi\:}}_{ij}=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[p]{1-{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{j=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{1-{\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{p}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{j}}\right)},\\\:\sqrt[r]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{j=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{r}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{j}}\right)},\sqrt[q]{{\text{l}\text{o}\text{g}}_{\rho\:}\left(1+{\prod\:}_{j=1}^{n}{\left({\rho\:}^{{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{q}}-1\right)}^{{\omega\:}_{j}}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}.

Step 8. Integration of preference values.

Combine the different preference values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{{Z}_{ij}=\omega\:}_{j}\dot{\psi\:}}_{ij}$$\end{document} of alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\Xi\:}}_{i}$$\end{document} into a collective value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Z}_{i}$$\end{document} using Eq. (20).20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Z}_{i}=\left({\mathcal{U}}_{ij},{\mathcal{E}}_{ij},{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}\right)=(p,q,r)-SFFWA\left({Z}_{i1},{\vartheta\:}_{i2},\dots\:,{\vartheta\:}_{in}\right)$$\end{document}

Step 9. Calculate score values.

Calculate the score value of the aggregated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(p,q,r)-$$\end{document}SFN \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Z}_{i}$$\end{document} using Eq. (21).21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:SC\left({Z}_{i}\right)={\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{p}-{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{r}-{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{q}$$\end{document}

If two alternatives have the same score value, we need to determine the accuracy using Eq. (22).22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:SC\left({Z}_{i}\right)={\mathcal{U}}_{ij}^{p}+{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{r}+{\mathcal{V}}_{ij}^{q}$$\end{document}

Step 10. Ranking.

Rank the set of alternatives based on their score values. The alternative with the highest score value will be considered the best option. The step-by-step pathway of the proposed MCDM model is shown in Fig. 4.

Fig. 4The flowchart of the proposed MCDM model.

The flowchart of the proposed MCDM model.
